Ibid. " M' Carter.'^ OliTer Carter, B.D. of Cambridge, was elected 
Fellow of Manchester College under Queen Mary's Foundation about the 
year 1570. He joined with Warden Herle and liia co-feliows in leasing 
the Clinpter land^ for Inrge fines and small annual payments in the ITtb 
B!i«abeth. {Lane. MSS., vol. vi. p. 225.) He professed, however, after- 
wards to be strongly opposed to the spoliation of the Collegiate estates, and 
importuned Lord Burgliley and Secretary Walsingham in 1576 to preserve 
the revenues when they were menaced by Herle the tool of the Court party, 
(Hitt. Manchester Foundation*, vol. i. p. 87,) and yet Herle was favourably 
regarded by Archbishop Parker, who recommended him as "a grave and 
priestly man," fit for a bishoprick. (Strype's Parker, vol. i. p, 406.) Carter 
found it necessary, however, to secure the interposition of Dcnn Nowell to 
rescue the College from destruction, and the Dean urged Burghley that 
Mr. Carter's great charges in the necessary suit which he bad commenced 
might be considered. {Ibid. p. 407.) As Carter was ihe only " Bachelor 
of Divinity" connected with the College he was doubtless the individual 
alluded to by Strype, {Ibid. vol. 1. p. 407,) who, on riding to a Chapel 
belonging to the parish of Manchester to preach there, about the year 
1574, was set upon in the way by a parishioner, who, taking bis borso by 
the bridle, drew his dagger, and beat and wounded the Preacher with three 
wounds ; and if his horse bad not broken out of the other's hands, in ail 
piiabability he bad killed liira. Nor was it the first time their Preachers 
had been so used. In 1578 he was again nominoted a Fellow in Queen 
Eliaaheth'a Charter of Incorporation. Hollinworth says he was "a learned 
man, and writt a book in answer to Bristow's Motives" (p. 87.) Sicbord 
Bristow was a scholar of Exeter College, Oxford, and in the beginning 
of Queen Eliiabetb's reign distinguished himself, along with Campion, 
as an impressive Protestant orator. In 1586, when the Queen visited 
Oxford, Bristow's eloquence seems to hare attracted her Majesty's notice, 
but be afterwards became a pervert of Cardinal Allen, and was bis "right 
hand upon all occasions,'' being an acute disputant and a very prolific writer. 
The book popularly known aa Dr. Bristow's Motives, although the title is 
more copious, (aeo Watts' Bib. Brilan.) was published in 1574, translated 
afterwards into Latin by Dr.Wortbinglon, a native of Lancashire, and printed

Campion the Jesuit especially mentioned Oliver Carter as a Lancashire 
Minister " that boasted much of his learning and laboured to win " the 
Homan Catholics. (Uollin worth's Jfancun., p. 04.) It ts clear that Carter 
was a man of extensive reading and wrote ably and strongly, though, upon the 
whole, temperately, against his subtle and harassing theological opponents. 
Ue thoroughly understood the points of difference between himself and tliem, 
and was not disposed to lessen their importance, but there is no evidence 
that he was a vain man or that he boosted of his attainments, altbougli he 
had to thank Cambridge and his own industry for possessing no mean 
store of leonting. He was denounced by the papists 03 one of the concu- 
binarian clergy, although the question of the marriage of priests was not 
the stronghold of those who maiutained the pontilical supremacy, hut this 
ban of Rome was not likely to meet with much favour from Oliver Carter, 
who regarded the Church of Borne as the wanton woman clad in scarlet, 
granting, rescinding, and withholding dispensations of marriage, bulls, and 
iDdulgcnces, to such persons and at sueh times only as seemed good in her 
sight. Bishop Chaderion appointed him in 1578 one of the five Modera- 
tors of the Monthly Lecture in Manchester, and the powers thus vested in 
him were little short of episcopal. (Dr. Hibbert Ware's ffiit. of MancA.

This mutual consent for matrimony was ratified and the sentence for the t»- 
lidity of the marriage Bolemnly decreed and continued by Chancellor Leche 
Id 1586. (Chadertons Liefer, vol. ii. p. 47.) The only issue of this re- 
markable marriage was Elizabeth Brooke, bom about 15d5, to whom, says 
Sir Jobn Horington, the Bishop " means to leave a great patrimonie," and, 
who in April 1616 married Torrcll Joceline of Oakington, co. Cambridge, 
Esq. She was one of the most accomplished, pious, and learned females of 
her age, having been carefully educated from her early youth by her grand- 
father Bishop Cliaderton, and distinguished by the ardour of her application 
to the study of poetry, history, and jihilosophy, as well as of foreign lan- 
guages, " her candle being lighted from the lamp of the sanctuary." Fuller, 
in his Worthies ofChethire, says " she was a virtuous gentlewoman of rare 
Bcconiplishmonts." Having a presentiment that she should die in travail, 
which unhappily was realised, sho wrote a treatise, entitled " The Mother's 
Legacic to her vnbome Cbilde," first published in 1625. The letter ad- 
dressed to her husband in the prospect of death, as well as the " Legacie," 
are both so touching and natural, so replete with practical wisdom and hal- 
lowed principles, that no one who is uot past feeling can read them without 
emotion. She was a devout and consistent member of the Church of England 
and fondly iillached to its prescribed formularies. From her pious counsel

Ibid. "3f' Sorrocolde a Preteher.'\ There is little doubt that this 
"Pretcfaer" was Thomas Sorocold, a native of Manchester, educated at 
Oxford, patronised by the Lord Keeper Egerton and Rector of St. Mildred 
in the Poultry, London. He was the author of "Supplicotions of Saints; 
a Booke of Prayers and Pniyses in foure partes ,- wherein are three most 
excellent Prayers mode by the late famous Queen EUiabeth." 24th ed. 1638. 
The author dedicates the edition of 1617 to Charles Prince of Wales, and 
luentiona that the prayers had been favourably received by his sister the 
Queen of Bohemia "long before the translation of her vnlo that chmate," 
which was in 1612. Nine short prayers begin with each letter of the name 
of Elizabeth, and there is a wood-cut of the Queen " in memoriam eteroam ;" 
and although the title-page would lead to the inference that the prayers in 
the third part, on the defeat of the Spanish armada in 1^88, for the success 
of the navy in 1596 and for her navy in 1597, had been written by Queen 
Eliiabeth, and in the preface they are apeciolly ascribed to her pen ; yet in 
page 225 it is added, " tliia part containeth prayers in the namea of divers 
parties of severall estates." Dr. Hook, the Vicar of Leeds, has reprinted 
some of these admirable prayers under the title of " Prayers for a Week," 
18mo. 1852, from the thirty-eighth edition of 1693, and names that they 
were published originally in 1616, which may be questioned. The author 
was a near relative (probably brother) of Ralph Sorocold of Manchester, 
merchant, a rich man, son of Thomas Sorocold deceased, who might be the 
Tliomas Sorocold of Salford, who was converted by the preaching of John

Bradford tlie martyr, and wlio was living in 1356. Id his Will dated 10th 
September 1503 this Ralph Sorocold desired that his body might be buried

time of divine service, and befjueathed his "patent of Christ Church in 
Oxford " and all his leases to his cousins Edmund Prcstwich Esq., William 
Fox of Rhodes Grent., Siraon Malone, and Adam Holme, for the hene6t of 
Kathcrine his vrife (afterwards married to Thomas Goodyearc of Manchester 
Gent, ; see page 128, Note.) and his eleven children. (Lane. MSS., vol. 
WillB, and xssi. p. 273.) The judicious distribution of Church patronage 
by the Lord Keeper Egerton ie mentioned by Sir Francis, afterwards Lord, 
Bacon in the letter accompanying his book " Of the Advancement of learn- 
ing," in which he says "few have like iise of learning or like judgment in 
learning as I have observed in your Lordship, not only in those places in the 
Church whicli hare been in your own gift, but also in your commendatory 
Tote, no man liath more constantly held let it be gieen to the most deiereing, 
detur digniori ; and therefore both your Lordship is beholden to learning 
and learning beholding to yon." This fotnily of Sorocold was of ancient 
descent and of good alliance, having intermarried with the families of

Midgley in Errmgden in the parish of Halifiix in the county of York, wBa 
born there, according Co computation, about the year 1 .530, and was probably 
educated, like many of tlie great northern Divinea of that period, at St. 
John's College, Cambridge. His zeal and talent in maintaining the doctrines 
of the Reformed Faith attracted the notice of Archbishop Parker, who in 1561 
collated him aa the snccessor of Mr. John Hampson, the ejected Vicar of Roch- 
dale, who had been n secret Roman Catholic. Shortly afterwards, the Arch- 
bishop having determined to estahlish a Grammar School in Rochdale, found a 
ready coadjutor in the Vicar, who gave a site, parcel of the glebe near the 
Church, 4th November 4th Elizabeth, and also stimulated his tardy Parish- 
ioners to erect the School -honse, in order that his Grace's endowment might 
be secured in perpetuity. In 1564 Bishop Pilkington, whose early life spent 
in the neighbourhood rendered him intimately acquainted with its character 
and wants, writing to Archbishop Parker, informed bim that all his Livings 
in Lancashire, viz. Whalley, Blackburn, and Rochdale, except the laat, « 
out of order, and that the Clergy not only performed their ordinary duties 
negligently, but very seldom preached to their flocks. At this time Pilking- 
ton was applying to the Archbishop for the Vicarage of Rochdale for a 
friend ; so that it is not improbable that Midgley contemplated its resigna- 
tion, having, like many of his brethren, become offended by the use of the 
Surplice and other articles of clerical apparel, because they had been long 
worn by the Romish Priests, altbougii some of them had been used in 
the church before either Pope or Popery had been known in England. 
On the 25tb October 1564, Pilkington, whilst at Rivington with his rela- 
tives, wrote to Dudley Ear! of Leicester, the patron of the Puritans, and 
observed, "1 marvel much that this small controversy for apparel should 
be 90 heavily taken," having the good sense to view the whole question 
as one of no material importance, aud yet urged Leicester to use his 
iulluoncB to have the vestments removed. Probably Pilkington's arguments 
had induced Midgley to waive his scruple and retain his benefice, as on the 
4th January 1564-5 he subscribed to the use of the ecclesiostical vestments 
in the Chnrch, although at no time a very cordial advocate for " y' typpet 
furryd with blocke conye, the sirplea and velvit coppe," and he would have 
been equally or better satisfied had Hooper's rather than Pilkington 's 
on the subject found acceptance in high places. At the same time the

privetl of hia Living by the Bishop of Chester, nfler haviog held it nearly fifty

et spontancam resignationem Ricardi Midgley, Clerici, ultimi Vicarii et

have resigned it altogether in favour of his son. 

Notwithstanding hia unhealthy views on some Church matters he was 
not exactly one of those impracticable men who required to have sermons 
enjoined him by authority, as he was a licensed Preacher in the Diocese in 
15D8, and in February 1601-2 "Preacher at Leighe," not improbably one of 
the Queen's four licensed Lancashire Preachers, hia exciting and stimulatiDg 
style of oratory being well adapted to rouse the people from their apathy and 
effectually to expose and resist the system of the Church of Rome. He 
seems to have frequented the various Churches, somewhat after the fashion 
of the old predicant Friars, as an itinerant Preacher, and to have been pre- 
sent during the reading of Common Prayer without taking any part in it 
himself — an unsatisfactory mode of proceeding, it must he admitted, but 
perhaps not altogether irreconcilable with his notions of the right of private 
judgment. There were some of the Clergy about this time whose views 
and proceedings were perhaps more extreme than his, and tho twenty- 
seventh English Cnnon was framed to meet their case, the Holy Commu- 
nion being prohibited " to any that refused to be present at public prayers, 
according to the order of the Church of England." 

Dr.Whitaker held his memory in great and just estimation, notwithstand- 
ing all his errors and unhappy contentions with the ruling powers, and there 
is reason to conclude that the whole of the ejttensive parish of Rochdale, at 
that time a wide waste of moral darkness and depravity, was pervaded and 
blessed not transiently by bis fervid [ireaching and pious and useful labours. 
(Li/t of Sir Geo. Raddiffe, page 4, where some of the misBtatemonls arc

Vicarage. In the same lease the Archbishop secured to the Vicar and

and were, generation after generation, practitioners of physic. 

The wife of Mr. Richard Midgley, the venerable Vicar, was buried at 
Rochdale 24th February 1603-4, and he himself found sepulture there on 
the 30th May 1609, aged about 79 years. His Will has not been discovered 
either at Chester or York, and be probably died rich in nothing but good 
works, having only his example and blessing to bequeath to his family. 
Oil the .5l!i October, 38th Elizabeth 1595, he alienated a small copyhold 
estate at Trough near Whitworth to James Chadwick of Tongend in Spot- 
land, Yeoman. {Lane. MSS. vol. vii. p. 8.)

Cortwright, the principles of his good patron Whilgitl, who did not merit

Ibid. " Tht Eatk of Tj/rone"] Hugh O'Neill, Baron Dungnnnon and 
Earl of Tyrone, commonly called by the aemi-regal title of Tbb O'Neill, 
was the claimant of the independent sovereignty of Ulster, nottvith standing 
the illegitimacy of his father. Queen Elizabeth granted him tho forfeited 
earldom and estates of his reputed grandfather, but conciliation had little 
effect, as he was never friendly towards English interests. Afler the dia- 
pcraion of the Spanish armada in 15SS, sonic of tho scattered vessels were 
shipwrecked on the northern coast of Ireland, and Tyrone, at that time 
professing great loyalty to the Quccn^ but suspected by Fi(2william the 
Lord Deputy of being in leaguo with Spain, hastily quitted Ireland and 
sought an interview with Elizabeth, removed hor prejudices, professed al- 
legiance to her, and obtaiitod a confirmation of all his patents and an assu- 
rance of royal favour. This increased the hostility of Fitzwilliara towards the 
Earl, which induced Captain Thomas Leigh (sec ante, pp. 101, 209, Jfotei), 
one of the Queen's Commissioners in Ireland, to write an impartbl account 
of the state of Irish affairs to the Queen. Notwithstanding this, the Irish 
Qovemmcnt distrusted Tyrone's loyalty, and all the frightful disturbances in 
Ulster were attributed to his agency. He expelled tho English from the 
fort of Blackwatcr, and attempted to vindicate his open rebellion on tho 
score of oppression and persecution. At this time he was clearly not only 
King of Ulster hut of Ireland, and as such he was proclaimed at Rome. 
Essex, at the head of 20,000 soldiers, was unable to vanquish the Earl, not- 
withstanding the judicious mediation of their mutual friend Captain Thomas 
Leigh. After Lord Mountjoy's vigorous efforts had weakened Tyrone's 
forces, and the army sent by Spain to his rescue had proved ineffectual, 
terms were at length mode by tho rebels with the Lord Deputy, and 
Tyrone, whom tho Queen called " a most ungmleful viper," obtained se- 
curity both for his person and estate. At this juncture Elizabeth died.
